k^{\prime}{\leftarrow}\{0,1\}^{2d_{2}}
uniformly at random.

7:

2. Encrypt
M_{o}
using
\mathsf{QOTP}
:

8:

Compute
M_{o}^{\prime}=\mathsf{QOTPEnc}(M_{o},k)
as:

M_{o}^{\prime}=\left(X^{k_{1}}Z^{k_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{k_{2d_{1}-1}}Z^{k_{2d_{1}}}\right)M_{o}\left(X^{k_{1}}Z^{k_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{k_{2d_{1}-1}}Z^{k_{2d_{1}}}\right)^{\dagger}.

9:

3. Encrypt
M_{c}
using
\mathsf{QOTP}
:

10:

Compute
M_{c}^{\prime}=\mathsf{QOTPEnc}(M_{c},k^{\prime})
as:

M_{c}^{\prime}=\left(X^{k^{\prime}_{1}}Z^{k^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{k^{\prime}_{2d_{2}-1}}Z^{k^{\prime}_{2d_{2}}}\right)M_{c}\left(X^{k^{\prime}_{1}}Z^{k^{\prime}_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{k^{\prime}_{2d_{2}-1}}Z^{k^{\prime}_{2d_{2}}}\right)^{\dagger}.

11:

4. Pad
M_{c}^{\prime}
:

12:

If
d_{2}<d_{1}
, extend
M_{c}^{\prime}
to
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d_{1}})
using the isometric embedding:

V:\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(d_{1}-d_{2})},

defined as:

V|\psi\rangle=|\psi\rangle\otimes|0\rangle^{\otimes(d_{1}-d_{2})},\quad\forall|\psi\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}}.

The padded matrix is:

M_{c}^{\prime\prime}=VM_{c}^{\prime}V^{\dagger}.

13:

Step 4: Spectral decomposition

14:

Compute eigendecomposition

M_{o}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|;\;\forall i,\lambda_{i}>0\;;\;r=\mathrm{rank}(M^{\prime}_{o})=2^{d_{1}}\;.

Set the support projector
\Pi:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\ket{\psi_i}\!\bra{\psi_i}
.

15:

Step 5: Support projector

16:

Define

\Pi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|

Algorithm 2

Quantum Anamorphic Encryption(
\mathsf{QAE}
) Part-2

1:

Step 6: Canonical Purification of
M^{\prime}_{o}
.

2:

Define

|\Phi\rangle=2^{-d_{1}/2}\sum_{j=0}^{2^{d_{1}}-1}|j\rangle_{M}\otimes|j\rangle_{B}.

3:

Compute

|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle=\sqrt{2^{d_{1}}}(I_{M}\otimes(\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}})^{\mathsf{T}})|\Phi\rangle

Then
\|\ket{\varphi_{M_o'}}\|=1
.

4:

7. Construct a contraction on
\textit{supp}(M^{\prime}_{o})
.

5:

Compute

(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}^{-1/2}|\psi_{i}\rangle\langle\psi_{i}|\;\text{on}\;\textit{supp}(M_{o}^{\prime}).

6:

Define the support-normalized operator:

V_{0}\;:=\;\Pi\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\;M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\;(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\,\Pi.

7:

Enforce contraction without trace renormalization:

\kappa\;:=\;\|V_{0}\|_{\infty},\quad\kappa_{\max}=\max\{1,\kappa\},\quad W_{0}\;:=V_{0}/\kappa_{\max};\quad(\text{so that}\quad\|W_{0}\|_{\infty}\leq 1).\;

8:

8. Halmos unitary dilation of
W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{F}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
be a
single-qubit
ancilla with basis
\{\ket{0}_{F},\ket{1}_{F}\}
.
Define the unitary
U_{BF}
on
\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{F}
by its
2\times 2
block form in the
F
-basis:

U_{BF}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}&\sqrt{\,I-W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}(W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\dagger}\,}\\[2.58334pt]
\sqrt{\,I-(W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\dagger}W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\,}&-(W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix},\qquad U_{BF}U_{BF}^{\dagger}=U_{BF}^{\dagger}U_{BF}=I.

Consequently,

\bra{0}_{F}\,U_{BF}\,\ket{0}_{F}\;=\;W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}.

9:

9. Prepare canonical purification.

10:

Initialize control and dilation ancilla:

|+\rangle_{R}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|0\rangle_{R}+|1\rangle_{R}),\qquad\mathcal{H}_{F}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2},\ \ |0\rangle_{F}:=|0\rangle.

11:

Create coherent superposition:

|\Psi_{\mathrm{r}}\rangle\;:=\;\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\,|0\rangle_{R}\otimes|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle\otimes|0\rangle_{F}\;+\;|1\rangle_{R}\otimes(I_{M}\otimes U_{BF})\big(|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle\otimes|0\rangle_{F}\big)\,\Big).

Algorithm 3

Quantum Anamorphic Encryption(
\mathsf{QAE}
) Part-3

1:

10. Reduce to
RM
and identify the off-diagonal.

2:

Trace out
BF
:

\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}\;:=\;\Tr_{BF}\!\big[\ket{\Psi_{\mathrm r}}\!\bra{\Psi_{\mathrm r}}\big]\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{1}{2}X\\
\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\dagger}&\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}_{\!R},

with

X\;=\;\Tr_{B}\!\Big[\,(I\otimes\bra{0}_{F}U_{BF}\ket{0}_{F})\,\ket{\varphi_{M_o'}}\!\bra{\varphi_{M_o'}}\,\Big]\;=\;\Tr_{B}\!\big[(I\otimes W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}})\,\ket{\varphi_{M_o'}}\!\bra{\varphi_{M_o'}}\big].

Using the identity in the Conventions and
\Pi^{2}=\Pi
,

X\;=\;\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\,W_{0}\,\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\;=\;\frac{1}{\kappa_{\max}}\,\Pi\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,\Pi.

3:

11. Dephasing (pinching) on
R
to set the covert amplitude.

4:

Define the single-qubit dephasing channel on
R
,

\Gamma_{\lambda}(Y)\;:=\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\,Y\;+\;\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{2}\right)\,Z_{R}\,Y\,Z_{R},\qquad\lambda\in[-1,1].

5:

Apply
(\Gamma_{\lambda}\otimes I_{M})
to
\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}
:

M_{a}\;:=\;(\Gamma_{\lambda}\otimes I_{M})(\Omega_{\mathrm{r}})\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X\\
\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X^{\dagger}&\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}_{\!R}.

6:

Compute

\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\bigl\|\,(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\bigr\|\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}).

and choose
\eta
such
\eta\geq 2\,\kappa_{\max}
.

7:

Choose

\lambda\;=\;\frac{2\,\kappa_{\max}}{\eta}\quad\text{(so $|\lambda|\leq 1$ whenever $\eta\geq 2\,\kappa_{\max}$)},

to obtain

\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X\,=\,\dfrac{1}{\eta}\,\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi.

Thus, in the
\{\ket{0},\ket{1}\}
block form on
R
,

M_{a}\;=\;\left[\ket{0}\bra{0}\!\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\;+\;\ket{0}\bra{1}\!\otimes\frac{1}{\eta}\,\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi\;+\;\ket{1}\bra{0}\!\otimes\frac{1}{\eta}\,(\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi)^{\dagger}\;+\;\ket{1}\bra{1}\!\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}.\right]

Note:
Since
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is Hermitian,
(\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi)^{\dagger}=\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi
.

8:

12. Apply a permutation unitary.

9:

Draw
\sigma_{l}
uniformly; let
U_{\sigma_{l}}
be the induced permutation unitary on
\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M}
, and set

M_{f}^{(1)}\;:=\;U_{\sigma_{l}}\,M_{a}\,U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}.

10:

13. Output.
Return
M_{f}^{(1)}
.

Remark (feasibility condition on
\eta
).

The choice
\lambda=\tfrac{2\kappa_{\max}}{\eta}
requires
\eta\geq 2\kappa_{\max}
to keep
\lambda\leq 1
.
Since
\kappa=\big\|\Pi(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\Pi\big\|_{\infty}
is known to the encryptor, one can always pick
\eta
large enough (or equivalently use a smaller effective
\lambda
after increasing the control pinching depth) to satisfy this bound and obtain the exact off-diagonal
\tfrac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma 9

(Existence and basic properties of defect operators,
[
81
]
)
.

Let
C
be a contraction on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.,

\|C\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Then
I-C^{\dagger}C\succeq 0
and
I-CC^{\dagger}\succeq 0
.
Consequently, the unique positive square roots

D_{C}:=\sqrt{\,I-C^{\dagger}C\,},\qquad D_{C^{\dagger}}:=\sqrt{\,I-CC^{\dagger}\,}

exist. Moreover,

D_{C}^{2}=I-C^{\dagger}C,\qquad D_{C^{\dagger}}^{2}=I-CC^{\dagger}.

Proof.

See Sz.-Nagy and Foias
[
81
, Chapter I, Sec. 3]
, specifically the definition of defect operators. Since
\|C\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have
C^{\dagger}C\preceq I
and
CC^{\dagger}\preceq I
.
Therefore
I-C^{\dagger}C\succeq 0
and
I-CC^{\dagger}\succeq 0
. In finite dimension,
every positive operator has a unique positive square root, yielding
D_{C},D_{C^{\dagger}}
with
the stated properties.
∎

Lemma 10

(Intertwining for defect operators (Sz.-Nagy identity) Page 6, Eq. (3.4),
[
81
]
)
.

Let
C
be a contraction and let
D_{C},D_{C^{\dagger}}
be as in Lemma
9
.
Then

C\,D_{C}\;=\;D_{C^{\dagger}}\,C\qquad\text{and}\qquad D_{C}\,C^{\dagger}\;=\;C^{\dagger}\,D_{C^{\dagger}}.

Proof.

This is a standard identity in the theory of contractions; see Sz.-Nagy and Foias
[
81
, Chapter I, Sec. 3, Eq. (3.4)]
. Consider polynomials
p(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}t^{n}
. For such
p
,

C\,(C^{\dagger}C)^{n}\;=\;(CC^{\dagger})^{n}\,C\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N},

by a simple induction on
n
. Hence for every polynomial
p
,

C\,p(C^{\dagger}C)\;=\;p(CC^{\dagger})\,C.

By Weierstrass approximation and spectral calculus, this identity extends
to continuous functions on
[0,1]
, and in particular to
f(t):=\sqrt{1-t}
.
Thus

C\,\sqrt{I-C^{\dagger}C}\;=\;\sqrt{I-CC^{\dagger}}\,C,

which is exactly
C\,D_{C}=D_{C^{\dagger}}\,C
.
Taking adjoints yields the second identity
D_{C}\,C^{\dagger}=C^{\dagger}\,D_{C^{\dagger}}
.
∎

Lemma 11

(Transpose preserves operator norm,
[
47
]
)
.

For any matrix
A
,
\|A^{\mathsf{T}}\|_{\infty}=\|A\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

See Horn and Johnson
[
47
, Sec. 5.6]
. Let
A=U\Sigma V^{\dagger}
be a singular value decomposition (SVD), with
\Sigma\succeq 0
.
Then
A^{\mathsf{T}}=\overline{U}\,\Sigma\,\overline{V}^{\dagger}
is also an SVD with the same
singular values. Therefore
\|A^{\mathsf{T}}\|_{\infty}=\|A\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Theorem 12

(Halmos unitary dilation is unitary
[
41
,
71
]
)
.

Let
C
be a contraction on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{B}
(i.e.,
\|C\|\leq 1
). Define the defect operators

D_{C}=\sqrt{I-C^{\dagger}C}\quad\text{and}\quad D_{C^{\dagger}}=\sqrt{I-CC^{\dagger}}.

The block operator on
\mathcal{H}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{B}
defined by

U\;:=\;\begin{pmatrix}C&D_{C^{\dagger}}\\[2.58334pt]
D_{C}&-\,C^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}

is unitary.

Proof.

Since
C
is a contraction, the operators
I-C^{\dagger}C
and
I-CC^{\dagger}
are positive semi-definite, ensuring that the defect operators
D_{C}
and
D_{C^{\dagger}}
are well-defined, Hermitian, and unique. To prove that
U
is unitary, we must verify that
U^{\dagger}U=I\oplus I
. Note that the adjoint of
U
is given by

U^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}C^{\dagger}&D_{C}\\
D_{C^{\dagger}}&-C\end{pmatrix},

where we have used the Hermiticity of the defect operators.

We rely on the fundamental intertwining identity for defect operators

CD_{C}=D_{C^{\dagger}}C.

(35)

This follows from the observation that
C(I-C^{\dagger}C)=(I-CC^{\dagger})C
. By the functional calculus, this commutativity relation extends to the square root function, yielding Eq. (
35
). Taking the adjoint of Eq. (
35
) yields the complementary relation
D_{C}C^{\dagger}=C^{\dagger}D_{C^{\dagger}}
.

Computing the block matrix product
U^{\dagger}U
, we obtain

U^{\dagger}U\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}C^{\dagger}&D_{C}\\
D_{C^{\dagger}}&-C\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C&D_{C^{\dagger}}\\
D_{C}&-C^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}C^{\dagger}C+D_{C}^{2}&C^{\dagger}D_{C^{\dagger}}-D_{C}C^{\dagger}\\
D_{C^{\dagger}}C-CD_{C}&D_{C^{\dagger}}^{2}+CC^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}.

The diagonal blocks simplify to the identity using the definitions of the defect operators:
C^{\dagger}C+D_{C}^{2}=C^{\dagger}C+(I-C^{\dagger}C)=I
, and similarly for the bottom-right block. The off-diagonal blocks vanish as a direct consequence of the intertwining identities derived above. Specifically,
C^{\dagger}D_{C^{\dagger}}-D_{C}C^{\dagger}=0
and
D_{C^{\dagger}}C-CD_{C}=0
. Thus
U^{\dagger}U=I
. A symmetric argument shows that
UU^{\dagger}=I
, concluding the proof.
∎

The
vectorization operator

\mathrm{vec}(X)
for a matrix
X\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
stacks the columns of
X
into a single column vector in
\mathbb{C}^{mn}
. For a matrix
X=[x_{1}\mid x_{2}\mid\cdots\mid x_{n}]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
with columns
x_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, the vectorization operator is defined as

\mathrm{vec}(X)=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{C}^{mn}.

Lemma 13

(
[
86
]
)
.

For matrices
A,B,X
of compatible sizes:

\displaystyle\mathrm{(i)}\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{vec}(AXB^{\mathsf{T}})\;=\;(B\otimes A)\,\mathrm{vec}(X).

\displaystyle\mathrm{(ii)}\quad

\displaystyle\Tr_{2}\!\big[\,|\mathrm{vec}(A)\rangle\langle\mathrm{vec}(B)|\,\big]\;=\;A\,B^{\dagger}.

Proof.

See Watrous
[
86
]
(Sec. 2.1.2) for the vectorization identity (i), and
[
86
]
(Sec. 2.2, Eq. 2.63) for the partial trace identity (ii). Note that Watrous defines the partial trace over the second system
\mathcal{Y}
as
\Tr_{\mathcal{Y}}(\text{vec}(A)\text{vec}(B)^{*})=AB^{*}
, which corresponds to our notation, taking into account the conjugate transpose.
∎

Lemma 14

(Purification trace identity used in
\mathsf{QAE}
)
.

Let
M\succeq 0
be a density on
\mathcal{H}_{M}
and let

|\varphi_{M}\rangle:=\sqrt{d}\,(I\otimes(\sqrt{M})^{\mathsf{T}})|\Phi\rangle
with
d=\dim\mathcal{H}_{M}
.
Then for any operator
Y
on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
,

\Tr_{B}\!\big[(I\otimes Y^{\mathsf{T}})\,|\varphi_{M}\rangle\langle\varphi_{M}|\,\big]\;=\;\sqrt{M}\,Y\,\sqrt{M}.

Proof.

By Lemma
13
(i) with
X=I
,

|\varphi_{M}\rangle=\mathrm{vec}(\sqrt{M})
.
Hence

(I\otimes Y^{\mathsf{T}})\,|\varphi_{M}\rangle=(I\otimes Y^{\mathsf{T}})\,\mathrm{vec}(\sqrt{M})=\mathrm{vec}(\sqrt{M}\,Y),

again by Lemma
13
(i).
Applying Lemma
13
(ii),

\Tr_{B}\!\big[(I\otimes Y^{\mathsf{T}})\,|\varphi_{M}\rangle\langle\varphi_{M}|\big]=\Tr_{B}\!\big[\,|\mathrm{vec}(\sqrt{M}Y)\rangle\langle\mathrm{vec}(\sqrt{M})|\,\big]=(\sqrt{M}Y)\,(\sqrt{M})^{\dagger}=\sqrt{M}\,Y\,\sqrt{M},

since
\sqrt{M}
is positive and hence Hermitian.
∎

Theorem 15

(Correctness of the off-diagonal block in the
\mathsf{QAE}
embedding)
.

In the
\mathsf{QAE}
construction, let
W_{0}
be the support-normalized operator

W_{0}=V_{0}/\kappa_{\max}
with

V_{0}\;:=\;\Pi\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\,\Pi,\qquad\kappa_{\max}:=\max\{1,\,\|V_{0}\|_{\infty}\},

so that
\|W_{0}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.
Set
C:=W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}
(a contraction by Lemma
11
)
and let
U
be the Halmos unitary from Theorem
12
.
Prepare

\ket{\Psi_{\mathrm r}}\;=\;\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\ket{0}_{R}\otimes\ket{\varphi_{M_o'}}\otimes\ket{0}_{F}\;+\;\ket{1}_{R}\otimes(I_{M}\otimes U)\big(\ket{\varphi_{M_o'}}\otimes\ket{0}_{F}\big)\Big),

and define the reduced operator on
R\!M
by
\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}:=\Tr_{BF}[\,|\Psi_{\mathrm{r}}\rangle\langle\Psi_{\mathrm{r}}|\,]
.
Then
\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}
has the
2\times 2
block form (with respect to
R
)

\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{1}{2}X\\
\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\dagger}&\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},\qquad X\;=\;\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\,W_{0}\,\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\;=\;\frac{1}{\kappa_{\max}}\,\Pi\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,\Pi.

Proof.

Expanding
|\Psi_{\mathrm{r}}\rangle\langle\Psi_{\mathrm{r}}|
and tracing out
BF
yields four blocks on
R
.
The diagonal blocks are equal by construction and satisfy

\Tr_{BF}\!\big[\,|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}|\otimes|0\rangle\langle 0|_{F}\,\big]\;=\;M_{o}^{\prime},\qquad\Tr_{BF}\!\big[\,(I\otimes U)(|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}|\otimes|0\rangle\langle 0|_{F})(I\otimes U)^{\dagger}\,\big]\;=\;M_{o}^{\prime},

by unitarity of
U
and invariance of the partial trace, hence each diagonal block equals
\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}
.

For the off-diagonal block, using
\bra{0}_{F}U\ket{0}_{F}=C=W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}
(Theorem
12
)
and Lemma
14
with
Y:=W_{0}
, we obtain

X\;=\;\Tr_{B}\!\big[(I\otimes\bra{0}_{F}U\ket{0}_{F})\,|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}|\big]\;=\;\Tr_{B}\!\big[(I\otimes W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}})\,|\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle\varphi_{M_{o}^{\prime}}|\big]\;=\;\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\,W_{0}\,\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}.

By the definition of
W_{0}
and the support projector
\Pi
,

\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\,W_{0}\,\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}=\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\Big(\frac{1}{\kappa_{\max}}\,\Pi\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1/2}\,\Pi\Big)\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{\max}}\,\Pi\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,\Pi.

This proves the claim.
∎

Theorem 16
.

Let
\Gamma_{\lambda}
be the single-qubit dephasing channel on
R
given by

\Gamma_{\lambda}(Y)\;=\;\left(\frac{1+\lambda}{2}\right)\,Y\;+\;\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{2}\right)\,Z_{R}\,Y\,Z_{R},\qquad\lambda\in[-1,1].

Apply
(\Gamma_{\lambda}\otimes I_{M})
to
\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}
of Theorem
15
and set

M_{a}:=(\Gamma_{\lambda}\otimes I_{M})(\Omega_{\mathrm{r}})
.
Then

M_{a}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,X\\
\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\,X^{\dagger}&\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and, with }\;\lambda\;=\;\frac{2\,\kappa_{\max}}{\eta}\ (\;|\lambda|\leq 1\ \text{whenever }\eta\geq 2\,\kappa_{\max}\;),

its off-diagonal equals

\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X\,=\,\dfrac{1}{\eta}\,\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi.

Proof.

The channel
\Gamma_{\lambda}
leaves the diagonal blocks of a
2\times 2
block operator (in the
R
-basis) invariant
and multiplies the off-diagonals by
\lambda
. Hence

M_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X\\
\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X^{\dagger}&\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

By Theorem
15
,
X=\frac{1}{\kappa_{\max}}\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi
.
Choosing
\lambda=2\,\kappa_{\max}/\eta
gives

\tfrac{\lambda}{2}X=\frac{1}{\eta}\,\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi.

Trace preservation and complete positivity follow from the CPTP nature of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
.
∎

Corollary 16.1
.

Let
U_{\sigma_{l}}
be any permutation unitary on
\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M}
, and set

M_{f}^{(1)}:=U_{\sigma_{l}}\,M_{a}\,U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}
.
Then
M_{f}^{(1)}
is a valid density operator with the same spectrum as
M_{a}
.

Proof.

Unitary conjugation preserves positivity, trace, and spectrum.
∎

Theorem 17
.

Under the
\mathsf{QAE}
construction with
W_{0}
a contraction on
\textit{supp}(M_{o}^{\prime})
, the Halmos dilation
U

of
C=W_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}
is unitary (Theorem
12
). The reduced state
\Omega_{\mathrm{r}}
on
R\!M

has diagonal blocks
\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}
and off-diagonal block

X=\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}\,W_{0}\,\sqrt{M_{o}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{\max}}\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi
(Theorem
15
).
Subsequent dephasing with
\lambda=2\kappa_{\max}/\eta
yields

M_{a}\;=\;\left[\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\;+\;\ket{0}\bra{1}\otimes\frac{1}{\eta}\,\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi\;+\;\ket{1}\bra{0}\otimes\frac{1}{\eta}\,(\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi)^{\dagger}\;+\;\ket{1}\bra{1}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\right],

which is a valid density operator. Finally, the permutation unitary
U_{\sigma_{l}}
produces

M_{f}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}M_{a}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}
, a valid anamorphic ciphertext
(Corollary
16.1
).

Now we describe the decryption algorithm to extract and decrypt the original secret from the anamorphic ciphertext:

Algorithm 4

Decryption of Original Secret from Anamorphic Ciphertext(
\mathsf{DOM}
)

1:

Input:
Anamorphic state
M_{f}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(d_{1}+1)})
, permutation unitary
U_{\sigma_{l}}
,
\mathsf{QOTP}
key
k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}
, dimension
d_{1}
.

2:

Output:
Original density matrix
M_{o}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{M})
.

3:

Steps:

4:

1. Apply the inverse permutation:

5:

Compute the intermediate state
M_{d}^{(1)}
by applying the inverse of
U_{\sigma_{l}}
:

M_{d}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}M_{f}^{(1)}U_{\sigma_{l}}.

6:

Quantum Dephasing Channel on Control Qubit

7:

Implement the dephasing channel
\Gamma_{0}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
:

\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{R})=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{R}+\frac{1}{2}Z_{R}\rho_{R}Z_{R}

and set

M_{a}^{(0)}\ :=\ (\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{2^{d_{1}}})\big(M_{d}^{(1)}\big).

(36)

8:

Step 3: Quantum Partial Trace

9:

Trace out the control register
R
:

\Tr_{R}\!\big[M_{a}^{(0)}\big]\ =\ M_{o}^{\prime}.

(37)

10:

5. Apply
\mathsf{QOTP}
Decryption:

11:

Use the
\mathsf{QOTP}
decryption key
k=(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{2d_{1}})
to recover
M_{o}
:

M_{o}=\mathsf{QOTPDec}(M_{o}^{\prime},k)=\Bigl(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{d_{1}}X^{k_{2j-1}}Z^{k_{2j}}\Bigr)^{\dagger}M_{o}^{\prime}\Bigl(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{d_{1}}X^{k_{2j-1}}Z^{k_{2j}}\Bigr).

12:

6. Return the original density matrix
M_{o}
:

13:

Output
M_{o}
, completing the decryption.

Theorem 18

(Correctness of
\mathsf{DOM}
Algorithm)
.

Let
M_{f}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M})
be an anamorphic ciphertext satisfying:

M_{f}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{\ell}}M_{a}U_{\sigma_{\ell}}^{\dagger}

where
U_{\sigma_{\ell}}
is a permutation unitary and
M_{a}
has the canonical block form

M_{a}=\left[\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+\ket{1}\bra{1}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+\ket{0}\bra{1}\otimes\tfrac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}+\ket{1}\bra{0}\otimes\tfrac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\right]

with
M_{o}^{\prime}=U_{k}M_{o}U_{k}^{\dagger}
for some key
k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}
,
U_{k}=\mathsf{QOTP}(\cdot,k)
and security parameter
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Then the DOM algorithm exactly recovers
M_{o}
.

Proof.

Applying the inverse permutation unitary

M_{d}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{\ell}}^{\dagger}M_{f}^{(1)}U_{\sigma_{\ell}}=U_{\sigma_{\ell}}^{\dagger}(U_{\sigma_{\ell}}M_{a}U_{\sigma_{\ell}}^{\dagger})U_{\sigma_{\ell}}=M_{a}

Thus, we recover the unpermuted anamorphic state

M_{d}^{(1)}=M_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

(38)

Let
\Gamma_{0}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
be the dephasing channel defined by

\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{R})=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{R}+\frac{1}{2}Z_{R}\rho_{R}Z_{R}

This channel has Kraus operators
K_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}I_{R}
and
K_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}Z_{R}
, satisfying the completeness relation:

K_{0}^{\dagger}K_{0}+K_{1}^{\dagger}K_{1}=\frac{1}{2}I_{R}+\frac{1}{2}Z_{R}^{\dagger}Z_{R}=\frac{1}{2}I_{R}+\frac{1}{2}I_{R}=I_{R}

The tensor product channel
(\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{M})
acts on the composite system as

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{M})(M_{d}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{1}(K_{i}\otimes I_{M})M_{d}^{(1)}(K_{i}\otimes I_{M})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}M_{d}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}(Z_{R}\otimes I_{M})M_{d}^{(1)}(Z_{R}\otimes I_{M})

We compute each term explicitly. First, note that

Z_{R}\otimes I_{M}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\otimes I_{M}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{M}&0\\
0&-I_{M}\end{pmatrix}

Then

\displaystyle(Z_{R}\otimes I_{M})M_{d}^{(1)}(Z_{R}\otimes I_{M})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{M}&0\\
0&-I_{M}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{M}&0\\
0&-I_{M}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{M}&0\\
0&-I_{M}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&-\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&-\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&-\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
-\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

Therefore

\displaystyle M_{a}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{M})(M_{d}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&-\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
-\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{4}M_{o}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}M_{o}^{\prime}&\frac{1}{2\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{2\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
\frac{1}{2\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{4}M_{o}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

Thus, the dephasing channel annihilates the off-diagonal blocks

M_{a}^{(0)}=\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+\ket{1}\bra{1}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}

(39)

The partial trace over the control register
R
is computed as

\Tr_{R}[M_{a}^{(0)}]=\Tr_{R}\left[\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+\ket{1}\bra{1}\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\right]

Using the linearity of the partial trace and the fact that
\Tr[\ket{b}\bra{b}]=1
for
b\in\{0,1\}
, we get

\displaystyle\Tr_{R}[M_{a}^{(0)}]

\displaystyle=\Tr[\ket{0}\bra{0}]\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+\Tr[\ket{1}\bra{1}]\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=1\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+1\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=M_{o}^{\prime}

By definition,
M_{o}^{\prime}=U_{k}M_{o}U_{k}^{\dagger}
, where

U_{k}=\mathsf{QOTP}(\cdot,k).

Applying the inverse QOTP unitary, we get

U_{k}^{\dagger}M_{o}^{\prime}U_{k}=M_{o}.

Thus, the original message is exactly recovered

\mathsf{QOTPDec}(M_{o}^{\prime},k)=M_{o}.

(40)

Combining equations (
38
) through (
40
), we have shown that the DOM algorithm transforms
M_{f}^{(1)}
to
M_{o}
through a sequence of mathematically exact operations, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 19

(Independence from Security Parameter)
.

The
\mathsf{DOM}
algorithm’s recovery of
M_{o}
is independent of the security parameter
\eta
.

Proof.

From equation (
39
), the dephasing channel
\Gamma_{0}
completely eliminates the off-diagonal terms containing
\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
and
\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}
. The remaining diagonal terms
\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}
are independent of
\eta
. The subsequent partial trace and QOTP decryption operations do not introduce any
\eta
-dependence. Therefore, the final output
M_{o}
is independent of
\eta
.
∎

Theorem 20

(CPTP Composition)
.

The
\mathsf{DOM}
algorithm is a completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) map.

Proof.

Each step of the DOM algorithm is CPTP:

1.

Inverse permutation
: Unitary conjugation by
U_{\sigma_{\ell}}^{\dagger}
is CPTP.

2.

Dephasing channel
:
(\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{M})
is CPTP, as shown by its Kraus operator representation:

(\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{M})(\rho)=\sum_{i=0}^{1}(K_{i}\otimes I_{M})\rho(K_{i}\otimes I_{M})^{\dagger}

with
\sum_{i}(K_{i}\otimes I_{M})^{\dagger}(K_{i}\otimes I_{M})=I_{R}\otimes I_{M}
.

3.

Partial trace
: The operation
\Tr_{R}[\cdot]
is CPTP.

4.

QOTP decryption
: Unitary conjugation by
U_{k}=\mathsf{QOTP}(\cdot,k)^{\dagger}
is CPTP.

The composition of CPTP maps is CPTP. Therefore, the entire DOM algorithm consists of CPTP maps.
∎

The covert secret extraction and decryption algorithm from the anamorphic ciphertext, is described below:

Algorithm 5

Decryption of Covert Message from Anamorphic Ciphertext (
\mathsf{DCM}
)

1:

Input:

•

Anamorphic state
M_{f}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{D}\!\big((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(d_{1}+1)}\big)
produced by encryption.

•

Permutation unitary
U_{\sigma_{l}}
used at encryption.

•

One-time-pad key
k^{\prime}=(k^{\prime}_{1},\dots,k^{\prime}_{2d_{2}})\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{2}}
for the covert message.

•

Dimensions
d_{1}\geq d_{2}
and scaling parameter
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

•

Isometry
V:\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}}\to\mathcal{H}_{M}
with
V\lvert\psi\rangle=\lvert\psi\rangle\otimes\lvert 0\rangle^{\otimes(d_{1}-d_{2})}
,

\mathcal{H}_{M}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d_{1}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}}=(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d_{2}}
.

•

Total number of copies (shots) for the probe:
N_{X}\in\mathbb{N}
.

2:

Output:
Covert plaintext density matrix
M_{c}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}})
.

3:

Registers and gates:

Let
R
denote the single-qubit control register;
M
the
d_{1}
-qubit register.
On
R
we use the Pauli operators
X_{R},Y_{R},Z_{R}
and the Hadamard
H_{R}
.
Define the computational-basis dephasing (pinching) channel on
R
,

\mathcal{P}_{Z}^{(R)}(X)\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}(X+Z_{R}XZ_{R})\;=\;\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}(\lvert b\rangle\langle b\rvert_{R}\otimes I_{M})\,X\,(\lvert b\rangle\langle b\rvert_{R}\otimes I_{M}).

Physical realization:

\mathcal{P}_{Z}^{(R)}
is implemented by measuring
R
in the computational basis and discarding the classical outcome (Lemma
5.1
).

4:

1. Undo the permutation unitary (one copy for illustration).

Apply
U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}
on
R\otimes M
to obtain

M_{d}^{(1)}\;:=\;U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}\,M_{f}^{(1)}\,U_{\sigma_{l}}.

By encryption correctness and Hermiticity of the covert block (Prop.
),

M_{d}^{(1)}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,B\\[2.5pt]
\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,B&\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}_{\!R},\qquad B\;=\;\Pi\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,\Pi,\;\;M_{c}^{\prime\prime}=VM_{c}^{\prime}V^{\dagger},\;\;B=B^{\dagger}.

(41)

5:

2. Real part via explicit measurements (X-probe;
N_{X}
copies).

For each shot
t=1,\dots,N_{X}
(on an independent fresh copy of
M_{f}^{(1)}
):

1.

Apply
U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}
on
R\otimes M
.

2.

Apply
H_{R}
on
R
.

3.

Measure
R
in the computational
Z
basis, recording
b_{t}\in\{0,1\}
.

4.

Perform an informationally-complete Pauli measurement on
M
: draw a Pauli string
P_{t}\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes d_{1}}
from a fixed design with known probabilities
\pi(P)=\Pr\{P_{t}=P\}
, rotate each qubit of
M
to the eigenbasis of its factor in
P_{t}
, and measure in
Z
, recording the eigenvalue
m_{t}\in\{\pm 1\}
(the outcome of
P_{t}
).

Algorithm 6

Decryption of Covert Message from Anamorphic Ciphertext (
\mathsf{DCM}
) Part 2 (Continued)

1:

3. Conditional linear inversion and unnormalized blocks.

Let
S_{X}(b)=\{t\leq N_{X}:\,b_{t}=b\}
,
n_{X}(b)=|S_{X}(b)|
. For each Pauli string
P
, define the Horvitz–Thompson estimator

\widehat{\mu}^{(X)}_{b}(P)\;=\;\frac{1}{n_{X}(b)}\sum_{t\in S_{X}(b)}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{P_{t}=P\}}\;m_{t}}{\pi(P)}.

Define the conditional linear-inversion estimator on
M
given
R=b
:

\widehat{\rho}^{(X)}_{M\mid b}\;=\;\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}}}\sum_{P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{\otimes d_{1}}}\widehat{\mu}^{(X)}_{b}(P)\;P,\qquad\widehat{p}^{(X)}_{b}\;=\;\frac{n_{X}(b)}{N_{X}}.

Set the
unnormalized
block estimators

\widehat{D}_{b}\;:=\;\widehat{p}^{(X)}_{b}\,\widehat{\rho}^{(X)}_{M\mid b}\quad(b=0,1).

(Under ideal dynamics,
\widehat{D}_{0}
estimates
\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{\eta}B
and
\widehat{D}_{1}
estimates
\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{\eta}B
.)

2:

4. Off-diagonal reconstruction and unpadding.

Compute

\widehat{\tfrac{1}{\eta}B}\;:=\;\frac{\widehat{D}_{0}-\widehat{D}_{1}}{2},\qquad\widehat{B}\;:=\;\eta\,\widehat{\tfrac{1}{\eta}B}.

Define the (padded) covert operator estimate
\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime\prime}:=\widehat{B}
.

Isometric unembedding:

\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime}\;:=\;V^{\dagger}\,\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,V\;\in\;\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}}).

(If encryption ensured
\mathrm{Im}(V)\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(M_{o}^{\prime})
, then
\Pi
acts as the identity on
\mathrm{Im}(V)
.)

3:

5.
\mathsf{QOTP}
decryption.

Let

U_{k^{\prime}}\;=\;\bigotimes_{j=1}^{d_{2}}X^{\,k^{\prime}_{2j-1}}Z^{\,k^{\prime}_{2j}}.

Output

M_{c}\;=\;U_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\,\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime}\,U_{k^{\prime}}.

Lemma 1 (Dephasing via measurement).

For any operator
X\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M})
,

\mathcal{P}^{(R)}_{Z}(X)\;=\;\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}K_{b}\,X\,K_{b}^{\dagger},\qquad K_{b}\;=\;\lvert b\rangle\langle b\rvert_{R}\otimes I_{M},

and equivalently

\mathcal{P}^{(R)}_{Z}(X)\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\bigl(X+Z_{R}XZ_{R}\bigr).

Thus
\mathcal{P}^{(R)}_{Z}
is realized by measuring
R
in the computational basis and discarding the classical outcome.

Proof.

Let
X
be a
2\times 2
block matrix on
R
and let

X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{00}&X_{01}\\[0.86108pt]
X_{10}&X_{11}\end{pmatrix}

with blocks in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{M})
.
Since
K_{b}=(\lvert b\rangle\langle b\rvert_{R})\otimes I_{M}
are orthogonal projectors with

\sum_{b}K_{b}^{\dagger}K_{b}=I_{R}\otimes I_{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{b}K_{b}XK_{b}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\lvert 0\rangle\langle 0\rvert\otimes I_{M}\bigr)X\bigl(\lvert 0\rangle\langle 0\rvert\otimes I_{M}\bigr)+\bigl(\lvert 1\rangle\langle 1\rvert\otimes I_{M}\bigr)X\bigl(\lvert 1\rangle\langle 1\rvert\otimes I_{M}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}X_{00}&0\\[0.86108pt]
0&X_{11}\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover
Z_{R}=\lvert 0\rangle\langle 0\rvert-\lvert 1\rangle\langle 1\rvert
, hence

Z_{R}XZ_{R}=\begin{pmatrix}X_{00}&-X_{01}\\[0.86108pt]
-X_{10}&X_{11}\end{pmatrix}

and therefore

\frac{1}{2}\bigl(X+Z_{R}XZ_{R}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}2X_{00}&0\\[0.86108pt]
0&2X_{11}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}X_{00}&0\\[0.86108pt]
0&X_{11}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{b}K_{b}XK_{b}^{\dagger}.

Thus
\mathcal{P}^{(R)}_{Z}
coincides with the measure-and-forget channel in the
Z
-basis on
R
, which is completely positive and trace-preserving. ∎

Lemma 21
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{M})
have the block form

M\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\[0.86108pt]
B^{\dagger}&A\end{pmatrix}_{\!R},\qquad A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{M}).

Equivalently,

M=A\otimes I_{R}+\operatorname{Re}(B)\otimes X_{R}-\operatorname{Im}(B)\otimes Y_{R}
,
where
\operatorname{Re}(B)=\tfrac{1}{2}(B+B^{\dagger})
and
\operatorname{Im}(B)=\tfrac{1}{2i}(B-B^{\dagger})
.

X-probe.

Apply
H_{R}
on
R
, then measure
R
in the computational
Z
-basis.
Let
p_{b}=\Pr(R=b)
, let
\rho_{M\mid b}
be the post-selected state on
M
conditioned on outcome
b\in\{0,1\}
, and define the unnormalized block
D_{b}:=p_{b}\,\rho_{M\mid b}
.
Then

\frac{D_{0}-D_{1}}{2}\;=\;\operatorname{Re}(B).

Y-probe (phase–Hadamard).

Let
U_{HS}:=H_{R}S_{R}
(Hadamard followed by phase).
Apply
U_{HS}
on
R
, then measure
R
in the computational
Z
-basis.
With analogous notation
E_{b}:=p_{b}\,\rho_{M\mid b}
for the unnormalized post-selected blocks,

\frac{E_{0}-E_{1}}{2}\;=\;\operatorname{Im}(B).

(In particular, if
B
is Hermitian, then
\operatorname{Im}(B)=0
and the
Y
-probe is
not
necessary.)

Proof.

Using

M=A\otimes I_{R}+\operatorname{Re}(B)\otimes X_{R}-\operatorname{Im}(B)\otimes Y_{R}
,
the
X
-probe conjugation yields

(H_{R}\otimes I)M(H_{R}\otimes I)=A\otimes I_{R}+\operatorname{Re}(B)\otimes Z_{R}+\operatorname{Im}(B)\otimes Y_{R},

since
H_{R}X_{R}H_{R}=Z_{R}
and
H_{R}Y_{R}H_{R}=-Y_{R}
.
Projecting onto
\lvert b\rangle
on
R
gives

D_{b}=(\langle b|_{R}\otimes I)\,(H_{R}\otimes I)M(H_{R}\otimes I)\,(|b\rangle_{R}\otimes I)=A+\operatorname{Re}(B)\,\langle b|Z_{R}|b\rangle+\operatorname{Im}(B)\,\langle b|Y_{R}|b\rangle.

Since
\langle 0|Z_{R}|0\rangle=+1
,
\langle 1|Z_{R}|1\rangle=-1
, and
\langle b|Y_{R}|b\rangle=0
, we obtain

D_{0}=A+\operatorname{Re}(B)
,
D_{1}=A-\operatorname{Re}(B)
, hence
(D_{0}-D_{1})/2=\operatorname{Re}(B)
.

For the
Y
-probe, with
U_{HS}=H_{R}S_{R}
, the conjugations are

U_{HS}X_{R}U_{HS}^{\dagger}=-Y_{R}
and
U_{HS}Y_{R}U_{HS}^{\dagger}=-Z_{R}
.
Thus

(U_{HS}\otimes I)M(U_{HS}^{\dagger}\otimes I)=A\otimes I_{R}-\operatorname{Re}(B)\otimes Y_{R}+\operatorname{Im}(B)\otimes Z_{R}.

Projecting onto
\lvert b\rangle
on
R
gives

E_{b}=A-\operatorname{Re}(B)\,\langle b|Y_{R}|b\rangle+\operatorname{Im}(B)\,\langle b|Z_{R}|b\rangle=A+\operatorname{Im}(B)\,\langle b|Z_{R}|b\rangle,

so
E_{0}=A+\operatorname{Im}(B)
,
E_{1}=A-\operatorname{Im}(B)
, and
(E_{0}-E_{1})/2=\operatorname{Im}(B)
.
∎

Remark 1
.

In our scheme the covert off–diagonal block is

B=\Pi\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,\Pi
with
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}=VU_{k^{\prime}}M_{c}U_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}V^{\dagger}
,
where
M_{c}
is a density operator,
U_{k^{\prime}}
is unitary (
\mathsf{QOTP}
),
V
is an
isometry, and
\Pi
is a projector. Therefore,
B
is Hermitian

B^{\dagger}=B
, and thus
\operatorname{Im}(B)=0
. Consequently the
X
–probe
alone suffices; we estimate

\frac{1}{\eta}B=\frac{D_{0}-D_{1}}{2}

and set
\widehat{B}=\eta\,\frac{\widehat{D}_{0}-\widehat{D}_{1}}{2}
.

Theorem (Exact correctness in the infinite–data limit.

Assume encryption produced
M_{d}^{(1)}
in the block form of (
41
),

M_{d}^{(1)}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\frac{1}{\eta}\,B\\[2.5pt]
\frac{1}{\eta}\,B^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}_{\!R},\qquad B\;=\;\Pi\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,\Pi,

with
B
Hermitian (as in the construction,
B^{\dagger}=B
). If Step 2 (the
X
–probe with explicit measurement) is performed with ideal operations and with the number of shots
N_{X}\to\infty
, then the estimator

\widehat{\frac{1}{\eta}B}\;=\;\frac{\widehat{D}_{0}-\widehat{D}_{1}}{2}\qquad\text{(hence }\widehat{B}=\eta\,\widehat{\tfrac{1}{\eta}B}\text{)}

converges to
\frac{1}{\eta}B
(hence
\widehat{B}\to B
).
Consequently,

\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime\prime}\to\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi
,

\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime}=V^{\dagger}\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime\prime}V\to M_{c}^{\prime}
,
and Step 5 returns
M_{c}
.

Proof.

Let
A=\tfrac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}
. By the Pauli block decomposition,

M_{d}^{(1)}=A\otimes I_{R}+\operatorname{Re}(\tfrac{1}{\eta}B)\otimes X_{R}-\operatorname{Im}(\tfrac{1}{\eta}B)\otimes Y_{R}
.
Applying
H_{R}
on
R
maps
X_{R}\mapsto Z_{R}
and
Y_{R}\mapsto-Y_{R}
.
Measuring
R
in the computational basis and discarding the classical outcome (i.e. the dephasing channel
\mathcal{P}^{(R)}_{Z}
) removes the
Y_{R}
–term and retains the
Z_{R}
–term. Denote by
p_{b}
the outcome probability, by
\rho_{M\mid b}
the conditional post–measurement state of
M
, and set
D_{b}=p_{b}\rho_{M\mid b}
(unnormalized blocks). A direct calculation then gives the probe identity

\frac{D_{0}-D_{1}}{2}\;=\;\operatorname{Re}\!\Big(\frac{1}{\eta}B\Big).

In our construction
B
is Hermitian, hence
\operatorname{Im}(B)=0
and
\operatorname{Re}(\tfrac{1}{\eta}B)=\tfrac{1}{\eta}B
. Therefore the target identity simplifies to

\frac{D_{0}-D_{1}}{2}\;=\;\frac{1}{\eta}B.

With ideal operations and
N_{X}\to\infty
, the empirical frequencies
\widehat{p}^{(X)}_{b}
converge to
p_{b}
, and the linear–inversion tomographic estimators
\widehat{\rho}^{(X)}_{M\mid b}
converge to
\rho_{M\mid b}
(entrywise, hence in any Schatten norm) by the law of large numbers. Thus
\widehat{D}_{b}=\widehat{p}^{(X)}_{b}\widehat{\rho}^{(X)}_{M\mid b}\to D_{b}
, and consequently

\widehat{\tfrac{1}{\eta}B}=\frac{\widehat{D}_{0}-\widehat{D}_{1}}{2}\to\frac{1}{\eta}B
,
whence
\widehat{B}\to B
.
Since unembedding
X\mapsto V^{\dagger}XV
and
\mathsf{QOTP}
decryption are completely positive trace–preserving (hence continuous) maps, we obtain

\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime\prime}\to\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi
,

\widetilde{M}_{c}^{\prime}\to M_{c}^{\prime}
,
and finally recovery of
M_{c}
.

\square

Finite–data accuracy and sample complexity (Pauli tomography;
X
–probe only).

Let
d=2^{d_{1}}
and fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
. In the
\mathsf{DCM}
procedure, the covert off–diagonal block is
B=\Pi M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Pi
, which is Hermitian by construction; hence
\operatorname{Im}(B)=0
and only the
X
–probe is required.

Theorem 22

(Finite–data accuracy and sample complexity based on Pauli tomography)
.

Let
d=2^{d_{1}}
and let
B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
be the (Hermitian) covert off–diagonal operator. Fix
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. Run the
\mathsf{DCM}

X
–probe only
as in Algorithm
5
: apply
U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}
, then
H_{R}
, measure
R
in the computational basis, and perform Pauli tomography on
M
with Pauli strings sampled uniformly across the
d+1
commuting Pauli frames (Fact
2
). Let
N_{X}
be the total number of shots, allocated uniformly over the frames.

If

N_{X}\ \geq\ \frac{(d+1)\,d}{2\,\varepsilon^{2}}\;\log\!\frac{4d^{2}}{\delta},

then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\Big\|\widehat{\frac{1}{\eta}B}-\frac{1}{\eta}B\Big\|_{2}\ \leq\ \varepsilon\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\|\widehat{B}-B\|_{1}\ \leq\ \eta\,\sqrt{d}\,\varepsilon.

Equivalently, there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that it suffices to have

N_{X}\ \geq\ \frac{c\,d^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\;\log\!\frac{2d^{2}}{\delta}.

Proof.

We consider the
X
–probe target as
T:=\tfrac{1}{\eta}B
. Using the Pauli orthonormal basis on
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
(Fact
1
), we expand

T\ =\ \sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{d_{1}}}\alpha_{P}\,E_{P},\qquad\alpha_{P}\ =\ \langle E_{P},T\rangle_{F}\ =\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\operatorname{Tr}(P\,T),

and define the linear-inversion estimator

\widehat{T}=\sum_{P}\widehat{\alpha}_{P}E_{P}
,
where, for each Pauli string
P
in a given commuting frame,
\widehat{\alpha}_{P}
is obtained from the difference of empirical means on the two post–measurement branches
b\in\{0,1\}
after the
H_{R}
gate

\widehat{\alpha}_{P}\ :=\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\frac{\overline{Z}^{(0)}_{P}-\overline{Z}^{(1)}_{P}}{2},

with
\overline{Z}^{(b)}_{P}
the empirical mean of
\pm 1
outcomes of
P
on the unnormalized reduced block
D_{b}
(see Lemma
21
). Each
\overline{Z}^{(b)}_{P}
averages
n_{s}:=N_{X}/(d+1)
samples (uniform allocation over the
d+1
frames).

By Hoeffding’s inequality for
\pm 1
–valued variables and a union bound over the two branches,

\Pr\!\Big(\Big|\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\overline{Z}^{(0)}_{P}-\overline{Z}^{(1)}_{P}\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\mu^{(0)}_{P}-\mu^{(1)}_{P}\big)\Big|\ \geq\ t\Big)\ \leq\ 4\,e^{-2n_{s}t^{2}},

where
\mu^{(b)}_{P}:=\mathbb{E}[\overline{Z}^{(b)}_{P}]
. Hence, for every
P
,

\Pr\!\Big(\big|\widehat{\alpha}_{P}-\alpha_{P}\big|\ \geq\ \frac{t}{\sqrt{d}}\Big)\ \leq\ 4\,e^{-2n_{s}t^{2}}.

(42)

There are at most
m=d^{2}
nonidentity Paulis. Applying a union bound over
P\neq I
in (
42
) with

t=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2n_{s}}\log\frac{4d^{2}}{\delta}}
,
we obtain, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\max_{P\neq I}\big|\widehat{\alpha}_{P}-\alpha_{P}\big|\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2n_{s}}\log\frac{4d^{2}}{\delta}}.

By orthonormality of the Pauli basis (Fact
1
),

\|\widehat{T}-T\|_{2}^{2}\;=\;\sum_{P\neq I}\big|\widehat{\alpha}_{P}-\alpha_{P}\big|^{2}\ \leq\ m\cdot\frac{1}{d}\cdot\frac{1}{2n_{s}}\log\frac{4d^{2}}{\delta}\ \leq\ \frac{d}{2n_{s}}\log\frac{4d^{2}}{\delta}.

Since
n_{s}=N_{X}/(d+1)
, the stated condition

N_{X}\geq\frac{(d+1)\,d}{2\,\varepsilon^{2}}\log\frac{4d^{2}}{\delta}

implies
\|\widehat{T}-T\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Finally, by the Schatten–norm inequality
\|X\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{d}\,\|X\|_{2}
and
\widehat{B}-B=\eta(\widehat{T}-T)
, we get,

\|\widehat{B}-B\|_{1}\ \leq\ \eta\,\sqrt{d}\,\|\widehat{T}-T\|_{2}\ \leq\ \eta\,\sqrt{d}\,\varepsilon.

This proves the claim. ∎

Remark 2
.

For low-rank/coherent structure, classical shadows or compressed-sensing tomography can reduce the scaling; these are implementable by random local Cliffords followed by
Z
measurements on
M
.

Next, we describe an algorithm to extract the original ciphertext from the anamorphic ciphertext, which we will use in the definition of anamorphic secret-sharing.

Algorithm 7

Extraction of Original Ciphertext from Anamorphic Ciphertext(
\mathsf{EOC}
)

1:

Input:
Anamorphic state
M_{f}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(d_{1}+1)})
, permutation unitary
U_{\sigma_{l}}
,
\mathsf{QOTP}
key
k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}
, dimension
d_{1}
.

2:

Output:
Original ciphertext
M_{f}^{(0)}
.

3:

Steps:

4:

1. Apply the inverse permutation:

5:

Compute the intermediate state
M_{d}^{(1)}
by applying the inverse of
U_{\sigma_{l}}
:

M_{d}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}M_{f}^{(1)}U_{\sigma_{l}}.

6:

Quantum Dephasing Channel on Control Qubit

7:

Implement the dephasing channel
\Gamma_{0}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R})\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
:

\Gamma_{0}(\rho_{R})=\frac{1}{2}\rho_{R}+\frac{1}{2}Z_{R}\rho_{R}Z_{R}

and set

M_{a}^{(0)}\ :=\ (\Gamma_{0}\otimes I_{2^{d_{1}}})\big(M_{d}^{(1)}\big).

(43)

8:

4. Apply the permutation to recover original ciphertext:

9:

Use the permutation unitary
U_{\sigma_{l}}
to recover
M_{f}^{(0)}
:

M_{f}^{(0)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}M_{a}^{(0)}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}.

10:

5. Return the original ciphertext
M_{f}^{(0)}
:

11:

Output
M_{f}^{(0)}
, completing the extraction.

Remark 3
.

The algorithm
\mathsf{DOM}
can be applied to the original ciphertext
M_{f}^{(0)}
too. Exactly in a similar way, we can retrieve the original message
M_{o}
from
M_{f}^{(0)}
.

Now we discuss the following theorems and corollaries to prove the Theorem
26
and the corollary
26.1
.

Definition 17
.

(Rayleigh Quotient [Section 4.2, Page 176,
[
46
]
])
Let
A
be a Hermitian operator on an
n
-dimensional complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. The Rayleigh quotient
R(A;x)
associated with
x\neq 0
is defined as

R(A;x)\;=\;\frac{x^{*}Ax}{x^{*}x}.

(44)

We now recall the fundamental
variational characterization
of eigenvalues of a Hermitian operator. This is sometimes called the
Rayleigh–Ritz theorem
(in finite dimensions).

Theorem 23
.

(Variational Characterization of the Extreme Eigenvalues
[
46
,
10
,
55
]
)

Let
A
be a Hermitian
n\times n
matrix (or Hermitian operator on an
n
-dimensional space). Denote its eigenvalues by

\lambda_{1}(A)\;\leq\;\lambda_{2}(A)\;\leq\;\cdots\;\leq\;\lambda_{n}(A)

(45)

ordered in a nondecreasing sequence. Then

\min_{\ket{v}\neq 0}\,R(A;\ket{v})\;=\;\lambda_{1}(A),\quad\max_{\ket{v}\neq 0}\,R(A;\ket{v})\;=\;\lambda_{n}(A).

(46)

In particular,

\lambda_{\min}(A)\;=\;\min_{\|\ket{v}\|_{2}=1}\,\bra{v}A\ket{v},\quad\lambda_{\max}(A)\;=\;\max_{\|\ket{v}\|_{2}=1}\,\bra{v}A\ket{v}.

(47)

The following Corollary
23.1
can easily be derived from the Theorem
23
, which we have used in our proof of the Theorem
26
. The Lemma
25
is also easy to prove using basic linear algebra, and we have used it to prove Corollary
25.1
. We have included them for completeness.

Corollary 23.1
.

Let
X,Y\succeq 0
be positive semi-definite matrices on the same finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. If

\lambda_{\max}(Y)\;\;\leq\;\;\lambda_{\min}(X),

(48)

then

(X-Y)\;\succeq\;0.

(49)

Proof.

Since
X,Y\succeq 0
, all their eigenvalues are nonnegative. To show that
(X-Y)
is positive semi-definite, we show that for all
\ket{v}\in\mathcal{H}
,

\bra{v}(X-Y)\ket{v}\;\geq\;0.

(50)

As
\lambda_{\min}(X)
is the smallest eigenvalue of
X
, and by the variational characterization of eigenvalues (or Rayleigh quotients), we have

\frac{\bra{v}X\ket{v}}{\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\;\geq\;\lambda_{\min}(X).

(51)

Since
X\succeq 0
, for any
\ket{v}\neq 0
,

\bra{v}X\ket{v}\;\geq\;\lambda_{\min}(X)\,\|\,\ket{v}\,\|_{2}^{2}.

(52)

Since
\lambda_{\max}(Y)
is the largest eigenvalue of
Y
, the Rayleigh quotient satisfies

\frac{\bra{v}Y\ket{v}}{\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\;\leq\;\lambda_{\max}(Y).

(53)

Similarly, for
Y\succeq 0
, we have

\bra{v}Y\ket{v}\;\leq\;\lambda_{\max}(Y)\,\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

(54)

Combining both the equations together, we get that, for any nonzero
\ket{v}\in\mathcal{H}
,

\bra{v}X\ket{v}\;-\;\bra{v}Y\ket{v}\;\geq\;\bigl(\lambda_{\min}(X)\,\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\;-\;\bigl(\lambda_{\max}(Y)\,\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\;=\;\Bigl(\lambda_{\min}(X)-\lambda_{\max}(Y)\Bigr)\,\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}.

(55)

By our assumption,
(\lambda_{\min}(X)-\lambda_{\max}(Y))\geq 0
.

Therefore,

\forall\ket{v}\in\mathcal{H},\quad\bra{v}(X-Y)\ket{v}\;\geq\;\Bigl(\lambda_{\min}(X)-\lambda_{\max}(Y)\Bigr)\,\|\ket{v}\|_{2}^{2}\;\geq\;0.

(56)

Hence,
(X-Y)\succeq 0
, that is,
(X-Y)
is a positive semi-definite matrix.
∎

Lemma 24
.

(
[
46
]
)
Let
X\succeq 0
be a Hermitian and positive semi-definite matrix. Then

\lambda_{\max}(X)\;=\;\|X\|,

(57)

where
\lambda_{\max}(M)
is the largest eigenvalue of
M
, and
\|M\|
is the spectral norm or operator norm of
X
.

In general, if
A
and
B
are two positive semi-definite matrices, then
AB
may not be a positive semi-definite matrix unless commutativity holds, that is
AB=BA
. But in our case we next prove that if
M_{o}^{\prime}
is strictly positive definite and
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is positive semi-definite, then the matrix
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is also positive semi-definite. Here we note that it is
not
necessary that the matrix
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
should be strictly positive definite.

Lemma 25
.

Let
Y\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
be a Hermitian positive definite matrix, and let
X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
be a Hermitian positive semi-definite matrix. Then

XY^{-1}X\quad\text{is a Hermitian positive semi-definite matrix.}

Moreover,
XY^{-1}X
is strictly positive definite if and only if
X
is invertible.

Proof.

For vectors
v,w\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we define,

\langle v,w\rangle:=v^{\dagger}w.

Then
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
defines an inner product on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Now as
X
is positive semi-definite, for all non-zero vectors
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\langle v,Xv\rangle\geq 0.

Since
Y
is positive definite, its inverse
Y^{-1}
exists and is also positive definite. The matrix
XY^{-1}X
is Hermitian since both
X
and
Y
are Hermitian.

Consider an arbitrary vector
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and also consider the
inner product
\langle v,XY^{-1}Xv\rangle.

Define
w:=Xv
. Then
\langle v,XY^{-1}Xv\rangle=\langle w,Y^{-1}w\rangle
. Since
Y
, is positive definite, for all
w\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
\langle w,Y^{-1}w\rangle\geq 0.
Therefore,
XY^{-1}X
is also a positive semi- definite matrix.

Note that
XY^{-1}X
fails to be strictly positive definite precisely if there exists a nonzero vector
v
such that

\langle v,\,XY^{-1}Xv\rangle\;=\;0.

(58)

However, we have

\langle v,\,XY^{-1}Xv\rangle\;=\;\langle Xv,\,Y^{-1}Xv\rangle.

(59)

Since
Y^{-1}
is strictly positive definite,
\langle w,\,Y^{-1}w\rangle=0
if and only if
w=0
. Hence,

\langle Xv,\,Y^{-1}Xv\rangle=0\quad\text{iff}\quad Xv=0.

(60)

Thus, if
X
has a nontrivial kernel, there is a nonzero
v
with
Xv=0
, leading to
\langle v,XY^{-1}Xv\rangle=0
. This shows that if
X
is not invertible, then
XY^{-1}X
is not strictly positive definite.

Conversely, if
X
is invertible, then
Xv=0
implies
v=0
. Hence the only way
\langle v,XY^{-1}Xv\rangle=0
can hold is if
v=0
.

Therefore,
XY^{-1}X
is always Hermitian and positive semi-definite. It is strictly positive definite if and only if
X
is invertible.
∎

Corollary 25.1
.

The matrix
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is a Hermitian and a positive semi-definite matrix.

Proof.

By applying the Lemma
25
. with
X=M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
and
Y=M_{o}^{\prime}
, the result follows.
∎

We now state one of the main theorems of our paper.

Theorem 26
.

Given a security parameter
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)>0
, with
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that
\dfrac{1}{\eta}<\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
both the original and the anamorphic quantum states
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
are quantum density matrices, if

\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\|M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}),

(61)

where
\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})
is the minimum eigenvalue of
M_{o}^{\prime}
on its support,
\|\cdot\|
is the operator norm and
M_{o}^{\prime}
is a strictly positive definite matrix.

Proof.

We consider both the block matrices

M_{a}^{(0)}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\mathbf{0}_{2^{d_{1}}\times 2^{d_{1}}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{2^{d_{1}}\times 2^{d_{1}}}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\;\text{and}\;M_{a}^{(1)}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}&\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\[6.0pt]
\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,\bigl(M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)^{\dagger}&\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},

(62)

where
M_{o}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{M})
is the
\mathsf{QOTP}
-encrypted version of a density matrix
M_{o}
. Since
\mathsf{QOTP}
preserves positivity and trace,

\,M_{o}^{\prime}\succ 0
and
\operatorname{Tr}(M_{o}^{\prime})=1
. Therefore, the matrix
M_{a}^{(0)}
is a density matrix.

The encrypted covert matrix
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{M})
is another operator obtained by encrypting the covert message
M_{c}
using
\mathsf{QOTP}
and then padding the encrypted covert message
M_{c}^{\prime}
. Since the matrix
M_{c}
is also a density matrix, and since
\mathsf{QOTP}
preserves positivity and trace,

\,M_{c}^{\prime}\succeq 0
and
\operatorname{Tr}(M_{c}^{\prime})=1
. As
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{pad}}(M_{c}^{\prime})\;=\;V\,M_{c}^{\prime}\,V^{\dagger}=M_{c}^{\prime\prime},
and
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{pad}}
is an isometry and completely positive by construction of
V
,
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\succeq 0
and
\Tr(M_{c}'')=1
. As
M_{c}
is Hermitian,
M_{c}^{\prime}
is also Hermitian, and consequently
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is also Hermitian. The parameter
\eta>0
is used to scale the off-diagonal blocks. It is clear that
\operatorname{Tr}(M_{a})=1
. Therefore, we only analyze whether
M_{a}
is a positive semi-definite
(M_{a}\succeq 0)
matrix. Writing

M_{a}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\[3.0pt]
B^{\dagger}&A\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{where}\quad A\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime},\quad B\;=\;\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime},

(63)

we note that

A=\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\;\succ\;0\quad\text{iff}\quad M_{o}^{\prime}\;\succ\;0,

(64)

which is true by hypothesis. The potential problem for positivity arises from the off-diagonal blocks
B
and
B^{\dagger}
.

By the Schur complement condition
4
for the positivity of a
2\times 2
block matrix, is that

A\;\succ\;0,\quad\text{and}\quad A-B\,A^{+}\,B^{\dagger}\;\succeq\;0\quad,

(65)

where
A^{+}
denotes the generalized Moore Penrose inverse on the
\textit{supp}(A)
. By the Theorem
3
. We have
(M_{o}^{\prime})^{+}=(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}
. Substituting

\,A=\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}
and
\,B=\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain

\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\;-\;\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr)\,\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr)^{\dagger}\;\;\succeq\;0\quad\text{iff}\quad\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\;-\;\tfrac{1}{\eta^{2}}\,(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})\,\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\Bigr)^{-1}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\;\;\succeq\;0.

(66)

We prove that a sufficient condition is to require

\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}),

(67)

where
\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})
is the minimum eigenvalue of
M_{o}^{\prime}
on its support and
\|\cdot\|
is the operator norm. Since
A=\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}
, and
M_{o}^{\prime}\succ 0
is invertible on its support, we can write

A^{-1}\;=\;\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\bigr)^{-1}=2\,\bigl(M_{o}^{\prime}\bigr)^{-1}\quad\text{on }\textit{supp}(M_{o}^{\prime}).

(68)

Let
B=\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
. Then
B^{\dagger}=\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}=\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
(since
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\succeq 0
, and
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is also Hermitian).

Hence,

B^{\dagger}\,A^{-1}\,B=\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr)\,\Bigl(2\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\Bigr)\,\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\eta}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr)=\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}.

(69)

Thus,

A-B^{\dagger}A^{-1}B=\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\;-\;\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}.

(70)

To ensure

\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\;-\;\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\;\;\succeq\;0,

(71)

we show that a sufficient condition, is that the largest eigenvalue of
\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}

does not exceed
the smallest eigenvalue of
\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}
.

Let

X\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime},\quad Y\;=\;\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}.

(72)

We want
(X-Y)\succeq 0
. We know that if
X,Y
are positive semi-definite, then

\lambda_{\max}(Y)\;\leq\;\lambda_{\min}(X)\quad\text{implies}\quad(X-Y)\;\succeq 0,

(73)

where
\lambda_{\max}
and
\lambda_{\min}
denote the maximum and minimum eigenvalues on the relevant support.

Now,

\lambda_{\min}(X)=\lambda_{\min}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{o}^{\prime}\Bigr)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}),

(74)

since scaling an operator by
\tfrac{1}{2}
scales all eigenvalues by
\tfrac{1}{2}
,

and

\lambda_{\max}(Y)\;=\;\lambda_{\max}\Bigl(\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr)=\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,\lambda_{\max}\Bigl(M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\Bigr).

(75)

Because
M_{c}^{\prime\prime},(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}
, are positive semi-definite and positive definite, respectively, by the Corollary
25.1
,
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is positive semi-definite, and by Lemma
24
, we get
\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)=\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|
, i.e. the spectral norm of that product.

Therefore, we need

\tfrac{2}{\eta^{2}}\,\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)\;\;\leq\;\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}),

(76)

which is equivalent to

\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\,\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\bigr)\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}).

(77)

Therefore,

\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\bigl\|\,(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\bigr\|\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}).

(78)

This condition forces
M_{a}\succeq 0
. Since
M_{o}^{\prime}
is a strictly positive-definite density operator,
\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})>0
. Since
(M^{\prime\prime}_{c})^{\dagger}=M^{\prime\prime}_{c}
, by making
\eta
sufficiently large, one can always satisfy
\,\tfrac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\|M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\;\;\leq\;\;\tfrac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})
.
Since the permutation matrices are unitary matrices, they preserve the positive semi-definiteness and unit trace. Hence, both the matrices
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
are positive semi-definite with unit trace. Therefore, both the original and anamorphic matrices are quantum density matrices.
∎

Corollary 26.1
.

(A weaker sufficient condition)
Given a security parameter
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)>0
, with
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that
\dfrac{1}{\eta}<\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
both the original and the anamorphic quantum states
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
are quantum density matrices, if

\frac{2\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})}\leq\eta,

(79)

where
\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})
and
\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})
are the minimum and maximum eigenvalues of
M_{o}^{\prime}
and
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
on their respective supports.

Proof.

If

\frac{\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})}{\eta}\;\leq\;\frac{1}{2}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}),

(80)

then,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})}{\eta}\;\leq\;\frac{1}{2}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})\quad\text{implies}\quad\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\frac{\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})}\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}).

(81)

We know that for any two matrices
A
and
B
of compatible dimensions,
\|AB\|\;\leq\;\|A\|\;\|B\|
.
Therefore, we get,

\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\;\;\leq\;\;\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\|\;\|(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\|\;\|M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|.

(82)

Since
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is positive semi-definite and Hermitian,
(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}=M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
and hence

\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\|=\|M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|.

Therefore,

\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\;\;\leq\;\;\|M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}\;\|(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\|.

(83)

Since
M_{c}^{\prime\prime}
is positive semi-definite, all its eigenvalues are non-negative, and the spectral norm equals the maximum eigenvalue
24
,

\|M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|=\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime}).

(84)

Similarly, since
M_{o}^{\prime}
is strictly positive-definite,
\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})>0
and hence,

\|(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\|=\lambda_{\max}\!\bigl((M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\bigr)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})}.

(85)

Combining the equations
83
,
84
,
85
, we get,

\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})}.

(86)

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\;\|(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}\,(M_{o}^{\prime})^{-1}\,M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\|\leq\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{\max}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime})}\leq\frac{1}{4}\,\lambda_{\min}(M_{o}^{\prime}),

(87)

which satisfies the sufficient condition we proved in Theorem
26
.
∎

\bullet

Adversary’s Observations in the Two Games

i)
The real game
\text{RealG}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\lambda,\mathcal{D}):

In the real game, only the original message
M_{o}
is encrypted and sent. We have the encrypted message
M_{o}^{\prime}=U_{k}M_{o}U_{k}^{\dagger}
by the
\mathsf{QOTP}
and the key
k
. Then we have constructed the following message
M_{a}^{(0)}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\mathbf{0}_{2^{d_{1}}\times 2^{d_{1}}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{2^{d_{1}}\times 2^{d_{1}}}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

The final state we have is
M_{f}^{(0)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}M_{a}^{(0)}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{C}).

ii)
The anamorphic game
\text{AnamorphicG}_{\mathcal{Q}_{a}}(\lambda,\mathcal{D})

In the anamorphic game, both
M_{o}
and
M_{c}
are encrypted and sent. The encrypted message is
M_{o}^{\prime}=U_{k}M_{o}U_{k}^{\dagger},\quad M_{c}^{\prime}=U_{k^{\prime}}M_{c}U_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}.
Then we have combined both the original message and the hidden message as
M_{a}^{(1)}=M_{a}.
Finally, we have constructed the state
M_{f}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}M_{a}^{(1)}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{C}).

In both games, the adversary receives the state
M_{f}^{(b)}
for
b\in\{0,1\}
, without knowledge of
l
,
k
,
k^{\prime}
,
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
,
\eta
. The expectation
\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(0)}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)}]
represent the average state the adversary would get if they sampled many ciphertexts using random keys.

Theorem 27
.

The expectations of the original and the anamorphic states are

\mathbb{E}_{l,d_{1},k}[M_{f}^{(0)}]=\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}+1}}I_{2^{d_{1}+1}},

(88)

where
I_{2^{d_{1}+1}}
is the identity matrix of dimension
2^{d_{1}+1}
and after considering expectation
\mathbb{E}_{k}[M_{o}^{\prime}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{k^{\prime}}[M_{c}^{\prime\prime}]
separately we get,

\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(1)}\bigr]=\alpha\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)\,I+\beta\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)\,J=\frac{2^{d_{1}+1}-1-\frac{2}{\eta}}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}I\;+\;\frac{2/\eta}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}J,

(89)

where the matrix
J
is such that
J_{i,j}=1
for all
i,j\in[2^{d_{1}+1}]
.

Hence the trace distance between the expectations is

D(\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)}],\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(0)}])=\frac{1}{\eta\,2^{d_{1}}}

(90)

which is less than
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
,

and

D(\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)}],\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)^{\prime}}])=0.

(91)

for any two density matrices
M_{f}^{(1)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)^{\prime}}
, when the randomization is taken over two different keys
l,d_{1},d_{2},k,k^{\prime},\eta
and
\tilde{l},\tilde{d_{1}},\tilde{d_{2}},\tilde{k},\tilde{k^{\prime}},\tilde{\eta}
from the same set
\operatorname{Sym}(n)\times[2^{d_{1}+1}]\times[2^{d_{1}+1}]\times\{0,1\}^{d_{1}}\times\{0,1\}^{d_{2}}\times\mathcal{J}
, with uniform distribution.

Proof.

We recall that the Quantum One-Time Pad (
\mathsf{QOTP}
) encryption of an
n
-qubit state
\rho
with key
k\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
is given by

\mathsf{QOTPEnc}(\rho,k)=U_{k}\rho U_{k}^{\dagger},

where

U_{k}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}X^{k_{2i-1}}Z^{k_{2i}}.
The expectation over all possible keys
k
is

\mathbb{E}_{k}[\mathsf{QOTPEnc}(\rho,k)]=\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2n}}U_{k}\rho U_{k}^{\dagger}=\frac{I_{2^{n}}}{2^{n}}\operatorname{Tr}(\rho)=\frac{I_{2^{n}}}{2^{n}},

(92)

since
\operatorname{Tr}(\rho)=1
.

Let
M_{o}\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d_{1}})
be an arbitrary
d_{1}
-qubit state (density matrix) representing the original matrix. The
\mathsf{QOTP}
encryption of
M_{o}
with key
k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}
is

M_{o}^{\prime}=U_{k}M_{o}U_{k}^{\dagger}.

We compute the expectation over all keys
k
,

\mathbb{E}_{k}[M_{o}^{\prime}]=\frac{1}{2^{2d_{1}}}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}}U_{k}M_{o}U_{k}^{\dagger}.

(93)

Using the properties of Pauli operators and the fact that the set
\{U_{k}\}_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}}
forms an orthonormal basis for operators on
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d_{1}}
(up to normalization), we can express
M_{o}
in terms of Pauli operators

M_{o}=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}c_{P}P,

(94)

where the sum is over all
d_{1}
-qubit Pauli operators
P
, and
c_{P}=\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}}}\operatorname{Tr}(PM_{o})
.
Then, we have

\mathbb{E}_{k}[M_{o}^{\prime}]=\frac{1}{2^{2d_{1}}}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}}U_{k}\left(\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}c_{P}P\right)U_{k}^{\dagger}=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}c_{P}\left(\frac{1}{2^{2d_{1}}}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}}U_{k}PU_{k}^{\dagger}\right).

(95)

Note that for any Pauli operator
P
(excluding the identity), we have

\frac{1}{2^{2d_{1}}}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}}U_{k}PU_{k}^{\dagger}=0.

This is because the conjugation of
P
by
U_{k}
effectively randomizes
P
over all possible Pauli operators, and their average is zero unless
P
is the identity operator, and for
P=I
, we have

\frac{1}{2^{2d_{1}}}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}}U_{k}IU_{k}^{\dagger}=I.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{k}[M_{o}^{\prime}]=c_{I}I,

where

c_{I}=\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}}}\operatorname{Tr}(IM_{o})=\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}}}\operatorname{Tr}(M_{o})=\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}}}.

Thus,

\mathbb{E}_{k}(M_{o}^{\prime})=\frac{I_{2^{d_{1}}}}{2^{d_{1}}},

and
hence,

\mathbb{E}_{k^{\prime}}[M_{c}^{\prime\prime}]=\,\frac{I_{2^{d_{2}}}}{2^{d_{2}}}\,.

Similarly, for the covert message
M_{c}\in\mathcal{D}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d_{2}})
, the encrypted state is

M_{c}^{\prime}=U_{k^{\prime}}M_{c}U_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger},

with
k^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{2}}
and we have

\mathbb{E}_{k^{\prime}}[M_{c}^{\prime}]=\frac{I_{2^{d_{2}}}}{2^{d_{2}}}.

We compute

\mathbb{E}_{l}[U_{\sigma_{l}}XU_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}],

for a fixed matrix
X
of compatible dimension. The set of all permutation matrices forms a group under multiplication.

Then we have,

\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)}]=\frac{1}{(2^{d_{1}+1})!}\sum_{\sigma_{l}\in\operatorname{Sym}({2^{d_{1}+1}})}U_{\sigma_{l}}M_{f}^{(1)}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}.

(96)

The representation of
\operatorname{Sym}(n)
is defined by
\pi:\operatorname{Sym}(n)\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
by

\pi(\sigma)\,e_{i}=e_{\sigma(i)},\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(97)

where
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is the standard orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
denotes the group of unitary operators on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, [See
[
32
,
76
]
]. It is well known that this representation is
reducible
.

Let

\mathcal{A}=\{A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{n}):\,A\,\pi(\sigma)=\pi(\sigma)A,\;\forall\,\sigma\in\operatorname{Sym}(n)\}

be the centralizer or the commutant of the representation
\pi
.

By the
Double Commutant Theorem
and by applying
Schur’s lemma
,
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{Sym}(n)}(\Phi)\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}=span\{I,\,J\}
, where
I
is the
n\times n
identity matrix and
J
is the
n\times n
matrix such that
J_{ij}=1
for all
i,j\in[n]
. Then, for any matrix
\Phi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
we have,

\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{Sym(n)}}(\Phi)=\alpha(\Phi)\,I+\beta(\Phi)\,J,

(98)

for some scalars
\alpha(\Phi),\beta(\Phi)\in\mathbb{C}
that depend linearly on
\Phi
. We will only denote
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{Sym}(n)}(\Phi)
by only
\mathcal{T}(\Phi)
if it is understood that we are considering the representation of
\operatorname{Sym}(n)
only.

For any matrix
\Phi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, the twirl

\mathcal{T}(\Phi)=\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[U_{\sigma_{l}}\,\Phi\,U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}\bigr].

We compute the coefficients
\alpha(\Phi)
and
\beta(\Phi)
in terms of two linear invariants of
\Phi
:

T(\Phi):=\operatorname{Tr}(\Phi)\quad\text{and}\quad S(\Phi):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Phi_{ij}.

(99)

Taking the trace of
\mathcal{T}(\Phi)
, we obtain

\alpha(\Phi)+\beta(\Phi)=\frac{T(\mathcal{T}(\Phi))}{n}.

(100)

On the other hand, the sum of all entries of
\mathcal{T}(\Phi)
is

S\bigl(\mathcal{T}(\Phi)\bigr)=\alpha(\Phi)\,n+\beta(\Phi)\,n^{2}.

(101)

The twirling map is
trace–preserving
. As the permutation conjugation simply reorders the entries, the permutation twirling
preserves the sum of all matrix elements
. Therefore,

S\bigl(\mathcal{T}(\Phi)\bigr)=S(\Phi)\quad\text{and}\quad T\bigl(\mathcal{T}(\Phi)\bigr)=T(\Phi).

(102)

Thus,

\alpha(\Phi)\,n+\beta(\Phi)\,n^{2}=S(\Phi)\quad\text{and}\quad n(\alpha(\Phi)+\beta(\Phi))=T(\Phi).

(103)

Solving both the equations
103
, we get,

\beta(\Phi)=\frac{S(\Phi)-T(\Phi)}{n(n-1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha(\Phi)=\frac{nT(\Phi)-S(\Phi)}{n(n-1)}.

(104)

Now, considering
\Phi=M_{a}^{(0)}
, we get

\alpha(M_{a}^{(0)})=\frac{2^{d_{1}+1}-S(\Phi)}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}.

(105)

Since
M_{a}^{(0)}
is diagonal and constant along the diagonal, its off-diagonal entries are zero so that

S\bigl(M_{a}^{(0)}\bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{d_{1}+1}}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{d_{1}+1}}(M_{a}^{(0)})_{ij}=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{d_{1}+1}}\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}+1}}=1.

(106)

and hence,
\alpha(M_{a}^{(0)})=\frac{1}{2^{d_{1}+1}}
and
\beta\bigl(M_{a}^{(0)}\bigr)=0.
Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(0)}\bigr]=\alpha\bigl(M_{a}^{(0)}\bigr)\,I+\beta\bigl(M_{a}^{(0)}\bigr)\,J=\frac{I}{2^{d_{1}+1}}.

(107)

Now, consider the quantum density matrix
\Phi=M_{a}^{(1)}
. Then, after taking expectation
\mathbb{E}_{k}[M_{o}^{\prime}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{k^{\prime}}[M_{c}^{\prime\prime}]
separately, we get,

T\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)=1\quad\text{and}\quad S\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)=\Big(1+\frac{2}{\eta}\Big).

Computing the coefficients
\alpha(\Phi)
and
\beta(\Phi)
, we get,

\alpha\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)=\frac{2^{d_{1}+1}-1-\frac{2}{\eta}}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\beta\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)=\frac{2/\eta}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}.

(108)

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(1)}\bigr]=\alpha\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)\,I+\beta\bigl(M_{a}^{(1)}\bigr)\,J=\frac{2^{d_{1}+1}-1-\frac{2}{\eta}}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}I\;+\;\frac{2/\eta}{2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}J.

(109)

Now, computing difference we get,

\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(1)}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(0)}\bigr]=\frac{2}{\eta 2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}(J-I).

(110)

It is well-known that, one eigen value of the matrix
(J-I)
is
(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)
and the eigenvalue
-1
has multiplicity
(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)
.

Therefore, the trace distance is

\displaystyle D\Bigl(\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(1)}\bigr],\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(0)}\bigr]\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\|\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(1)}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(0)}\bigr]\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{2}{\eta 2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}\|(J-I)\|_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{4}{\eta\,2^{d_{1}+1}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)}(2^{d_{1}+1}-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\eta\,2^{d_{1}}}.

(111)

Hence,
D\Bigl(\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(1)}\bigr],\mathbb{E}_{l}\bigl[M_{f}^{(0)}\bigr]\Bigr)<\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Choosing two different keys
(l,d_{1},d_{2},k,k^{\prime},\eta)
and
(\tilde{l},\tilde{d_{1}},\tilde{d_{2}},\tilde{k},\tilde{k^{\prime}},\tilde{\eta})
from the same set
\operatorname{Sym}(n)\times[2^{d_{1}+1}]\times[2^{d_{1}+1}]\times\{0,1\}^{d_{1}}\times\{0,1\}^{d_{2}}\times\mathcal{J}
, with uniform distribution, it is easy to see that
D(\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)}],\mathbb{E}_{l}[M_{f}^{(1)^{\prime}}])=0.

∎

Now, we prove the computational indistinguishability of the original and anamorphic ciphertexts.

Theorem 28
.

The original and anamorphic ciphertexts
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
are computationally indistinguishable.

Proof.

In the real game, the adversary receives the original ciphertext

M_{f}^{(0)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}.

(112)

and in the anamorphic game, the adversary receives the following anamorphic ciphertext

M_{f}^{(1)}=U_{\sigma_{l}}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}&\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
(\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&\frac{1}{2}M_{o}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger}.

(113)

We know the following inequality between adversarial advantage and the trace distance
\operatorname{Adv}_{\mathcal{D}}(\lambda)\leq D(M_{f}^{(0)},M_{f}^{(1)})

1
.

Now, we compute the trace distance between the real and the anamorphic ciphertexts,

\displaystyle D(M_{f}^{(0)},M_{f}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\norm{M_{f}^{(0)} - M_{f}^{(1)} }_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\norm{U_{\sigma_{l}}\begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{2} M_o' & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{1}{2} M_o' \end{pmatrix}U_{\sigma_{l}}^{\dagger} - U_{\sigma_l} \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{2} M_o' & \frac{1}{\eta}M_c'' \notag\\ \frac{1}{\eta}(M_c'')^\dagger& \frac{1}{2} M_o' \end{pmatrix} U_{\sigma_l}^\dagger}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\norm{ U_{\sigma_l} \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -\frac{1}{\eta}M_c'' \\ -\frac{1}{\eta}(M_c'')^\dagger& 0 \end{pmatrix} U_{\sigma_l}^\dagger}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\norm{ \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -\frac{1}{\eta}M_c'' \\ -\frac{1}{\eta}(M_c'')^\dagger& 0 \end{pmatrix} }_{1}(\text{
since $U_{\sigma_{l}}$ is unitary and the trace norm is unitary invariant}).

(114)

As,
M_{c}
is a Hermitian, positive semi-definite matrix with
\operatorname{Tr}(M_{c})=1
, after encrypting with the key
k^{\prime}
the density matrix
M_{c}^{\prime}=\mathsf{QOTP}(M_{c},k^{\prime})
, remains as Hermitian, positive semi-definite and preserves the norm. Hence,
\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}
.

Denote the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{\eta}M_{c}^{\prime\prime}\\
-\frac{1}{\eta}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{\dagger}&0\end{pmatrix}
by
A
.

Then

A^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{2}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\eta^{2}}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(115)

The trace norm of
A
is

\|A\|_{1}=\operatorname{Tr}(\sqrt{A^{2}})=\frac{2}{\eta}.\operatorname{Tr}(M_{c}^{\prime\prime})=\frac{2}{\eta}.

(116)

Thus, the trace distance is

D(M_{f}^{(0)},M_{f}^{(1)})=\dfrac{1}{2}\|M_{f}^{(0)}-M_{f}^{(1)}\|_{1}=\dfrac{1}{2}\|A\|_{1}=\dfrac{1}{2}\cdot\dfrac{2}{\eta}=\dfrac{1}{\eta}<\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

(117)

∎

Note that as we have remarked earlier
3
that the algorithm
\mathsf{DOM}
can be applied to both
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
. The dictator and the players can decryprt the original ciphertext
M_{o}
from both the ciphertexts
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
exactly using the same
\mathsf{DOM}
algorithm. Therefore, both the ciphertexts
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
are indistinguishable to the dictator.

In quantum mechanics, fidelity is a metric used to quantify the similarity or closeness between quantum states. A high fidelity value indicates that the states are nearly identical. In our case, as a consequence of negligible trace distance, it is easy to show that the original and anamorphic quantum states exhibit a high fidelity. Consequently, this establishes that, in our case, an adversary or dictator is computationally unable to distinguish between the ciphertexts
M_{f}^{(0)}
and
M_{f}^{(1)}
, making it infeasible to identify which corresponds to the original ciphertext.

Theorem 29
.

The fidelity between the original and the anamorphic states is

F(M_{f}^{(0)},M_{f}^{(1)})\geq\Bigg(1-\dfrac{1}{\eta}\Bigg)

indicating that the two states are nearly indistinguishable for large
\eta
.

Proof.

By the Fuchs-van de Graaf inequality, and Theorem
28
,
\left(1-F(M_{f}^{(1)},M_{f}^{(0)})\right)\leq\frac{1}{\eta}
.

Hence,

F(M_{f}^{(1)},M_{f}^{(0)})\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\right).

∎

Now we describe the communication procedure between Alice and Bob under dictatorial supervision.

Algorithm 8

Transmission Protocol under Dictatorial Supervision (TPDS)

1:

Input:
Anamorphic state
M_{f}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{L}((\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(d_{1}+1)})
, dimensions
d_{1},d_{2}
, keys
k,k^{\prime}
, and permutation matrix
U_{\sigma_{l}}
.

2:

Output:
The original message
M_{o}
for both Bob and the dictator, and the covert message
M_{c}
exclusively for Bob.

3:

Step 1: Alice’s Transmission to Bob:

4:

Alice generates the anamorphic encrypted state
M_{f}^{(1)}
using the encryption process.

5:

Alice transmits:

M_{f}^{(1)},\quad(l,d_{1},d_{2},k,k^{\prime},\eta)

securely to Bob.

6:

Step 2: Bob’s Decryption:

7:

Bob receives
M_{f}^{(1)}
and the keys
l,d_{1},d_{2},k,k^{\prime},\eta
.

8:

Bob performs the following operations:

1.

Run the
Decryption of Original Message (
\mathsf{DOM}
)
algorithm with the key
k
to recover the original message
M_{o}
.

2.

Run the
Covert Decryption of Anamorphic Message (
\mathsf{DCM}
)
algorithm with key
k^{\prime}
to recover the covert message
M_{c}
.

9:

Step 3: Bob’s Forwarding to the Dictator:

10:

Bob forwards to the dictator the following information:

M_{f}^{(1)},\quad(l,d_{1},k).

11:

Step 4: Dictator’s Decryption:

12:

The dictator receives
M_{f}^{(1)}
and keys
l,d_{1},k
.

13:

The dictator runs the
Decryption of Original Message (
\mathsf{DOM}
)
algorithm using key
k
to recover the original message
M_{o}
.

14:

Step 6: Output:

15:

Bob receives both
M_{o}
and
M_{c}
.

16:

The dictator receives only
M_{o}
.

Lemma 30

(Pauli Twirl /
1
-Design Property,
[
90
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{d}
be the Pauli group on
d
qubits. For any density operator
\rho
on
\mathcal{H}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes d}
, averaging over the group yields the maximally mixed state

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}_{d}|}\sum_{U\in\mathcal{P}_{d}}U\rho U^{\dagger}\;=\;\Tr(\rho)\frac{I}{2^{d}}.

Consequently, the channel
\Phi(\rho)=\mathbb{E}_{k}[\mathsf{QOTPEnc}(\rho,k)]
maps every state
\rho
(with
\Tr(\rho)=1
) to the fixed state
I/2^{d}
, effectively destroying all information about
\rho
.

Lemma 31
.

For any
M_{c}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{M_{c}})
, uniform
k^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{2}}
, and the fixed padding isometry
V
,

\mathbb{E}_{k^{\prime}}\big[V\,\mathsf{QOTPEnc}(M_{c},k^{\prime})\,V^{\dagger}\big]\;=\;2^{-d_{2}}\,\Pi_{V},\qquad\Pi_{V}=VV^{\dagger},

which is independent of
M_{c}
.

The lemma is immediate from the Pauli twirl identity (
30
) and
8
.

Lemma 32
.

Fix
\eta\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
according to Theorem
26
. For any inputs
(M_{o},M_{c})
, sampling fresh uniform
(k,k^{\prime})
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{k,k^{\prime}}\big[M_{a}(M_{o},M_{c};k,k^{\prime})\big]

\displaystyle=\;\lvert 0\rangle\langle 0\rvert\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 2^{-d_{1}}I_{2^{d_{1}}}\;+\;\lvert 0\rangle\langle 1\rvert\otimes\tfrac{1}{\eta}\cdot 2^{-d_{2}}\,\Pi_{V}

(118)

\displaystyle\hskip 35.56593pt+\;\lvert 1\rangle\langle 0\rvert\otimes\tfrac{1}{\eta}\cdot 2^{-d_{2}}\,\Pi_{V}\;+\;\lvert 1\rangle\langle 1\rvert\otimes\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 2^{-d_{1}}I_{2^{d_{1}}},

which is independent of
M_{o}
and
M_{c}
.

Proof.

Applying
93
to both diagonal blocks of (
12
), and Lemma
31
to the off-diagonal blocks (together with Hermitian conjugation for the
(1,0)
block). Linearity of expectation yields the claim.
∎

5.2
Perfect
qIND–qCPA
Security

Theorem 33

(Information-theoretic
qIND–qCPA
security of
\mathsf{QAE}
)
.

Consider the symmetric-key construction
\mathsf{QAE}
that, for each encryption, samples uniform keys
k\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{1}}
,
k^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2d_{2}}
and an uniform permutation
\sigma_{l}\in\mathrm{Sym}(2^{d_{1}+1})
, and outputs the ciphertext (
14
).
Then for any (possibly unbounded) quantum adversary
\mathcal{A}
as in Definition
16
,

\mathrm{Adv}^{\mathrm{qIND\text{-}qCPA}}_{\mathsf{QAE}}(\mathcal{A})\;=\;0.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the oracle. We show that
\mathcal{E}
is a
constant
channel, i.e. it maps every input to the same density operator on
\mathcal{H}_{RM}
, independent of
(M_{o},M_{c})
.

Fix an arbitrary input state
\rho_{E\,M_{o}\,M_{c}}
